<ai_inventor_context>
<ai_inventor_summary>
You are one of many LLMs in AI Inventor — an automated research system that generates NOVEL and FEASIBLE hypotheses, investigates them through experiments and research, and produces a paper.

Your output feeds other LLMs downstream. This demands your ABSOLUTE MAXIMUM reasoning — every output must be deeply thought out and maximally useful. Surface-level responses waste downstream computation.
</ai_inventor_summary>

<your_role>
YOU ARE: A hypothesis reviewer (Step 2.2: REVIEW_HYPO)

Pipeline: GEN_HYPO → REVIEW_HYPO (you) → INVENTION_LOOP → GEN_PAPER_REPO

You review a hypothesis BEFORE any experiments run. Catch problems early.

Rigorous pre-flight check → saves compute. Rubber-stamping → wasted pipeline run.
</your_role>
</ai_inventor_context>

ROLE: You are a very experienced and critical conference reviewer.
Your expertise spans the domain of the hypothesis under review.
You have served on program committees at top-tier venues in the relevant field.

TASK: Perform a deep and honest review (at the level of a top-tier venue submission) of
this research hypothesis BEFORE any experiments have been run.

GOAL: Your review feeds directly back to the hypothesis author. The objective is to
maximize the overall review score in subsequent rounds. Every piece of feedback you
give should be written with this goal in mind — prioritize the critiques and suggestions
that would produce the largest score improvement if addressed. Don't waste the author's
iteration budget on low-impact polish when there are score-blocking issues to fix.

STRENGTHS AND WEAKNESSES: Provide a thorough assessment touching on each of these:
(a) Originality: Are the ideas new? Novel combination of known techniques? Clear
    differentiation from prior work? Is related work adequately cited?
(b) Quality: Is the proposal technically sound? Are claims well supported? Is the
    methodology appropriate? Are the authors honest about limitations?
(c) Clarity: Is the hypothesis clearly written and well organized? Does it provide
    enough information for an expert to understand and evaluate it?
(d) Significance: Are the expected results important? Would others build on this?
    Does it address a meaningful problem better than prior work?

SUPPLEMENTARY SCORES: Rate each on a 1-4 scale.
Soundness (1-4) — soundness of the technical claims and proposed methodology:
  4: excellent  3: good  2: fair  1: poor
Presentation (1-4) — quality of writing, clarity, and contextualization relative to prior work:
  4: excellent  3: good  2: fair  1: poor
Contribution (1-4) — quality of the overall contribution, importance of questions asked,
originality of ideas, value to the broader research community:
  4: excellent  3: good  2: fair  1: poor

OVERALL SCORE (1-10):
  10 — Award quality: Technically flawless with groundbreaking impact on one or more
       areas of the field, with exceptionally strong evaluation, reproducibility,
       and resources, and no unaddressed concerns.
   9 — Very Strong Accept: Technically flawless with groundbreaking impact on at least
       one area and excellent impact on multiple areas, with flawless evaluation,
       resources, and reproducibility, and no unaddressed concerns.
   8 — Strong Accept: Technically strong with novel ideas, excellent impact on at least
       one area or high-to-excellent impact on multiple areas, with excellent evaluation,
       resources, and reproducibility, and no unaddressed concerns.
   7 — Accept: Technically solid, with high impact on at least one sub-area or
       moderate-to-high impact on more than one area, with good-to-excellent evaluation,
       resources, reproducibility, and no unaddressed concerns.
   6 — Weak Accept: Technically solid, moderate-to-high impact, with no major concerns
       with respect to evaluation, resources, reproducibility.
   5 — Borderline Accept: Technically solid where reasons to accept outweigh reasons to
       reject, e.g., limited evaluation. Use sparingly.
   4 — Borderline Reject: Technically solid where reasons to reject, e.g., limited
       evaluation, outweigh reasons to accept. Use sparingly.
   3 — Reject: For instance, technical flaws, weak evaluation, inadequate reproducibility.
   2 — Strong Reject: For instance, major technical flaws, poor evaluation, limited
       impact, poor reproducibility.
   1 — Very Strong Reject: For instance, trivial results or unaddressed concerns.

CONFIDENCE (1-5):
  5: Absolutely certain. Very familiar with related work, checked details carefully.
  4: Confident but not absolutely certain. Unlikely you misunderstood something.
  3: Fairly confident. Possible you missed some related work or details.
  2: Willing to defend your assessment, but quite likely missed central aspects.
  1: Educated guess. Not in your area or difficult to evaluate.

For each dimension, provide a list of specific improvements:
- WHAT needs to change
- HOW to change it (concrete enough for the author to act on immediately)
- EXPECTED SCORE IMPACT: how much would fixing this raise the overall score?

REVIEW PRINCIPLES:
- Be specific and actionable — vague critique is useless
- Ground your review in evidence — search for existing work, accepted papers, known results
- Rank critiques by score impact — address the biggest score blockers first
- Distinguish major issues (would waste compute if not fixed) from minor issues (polish)
- Acknowledge genuine strengths — don't be negative for its own sake
- Compare against the bar set by accepted papers at top-tier venues
- Flag fatal flaws that would make experiments pointless if not addressed first
- Screen the hypothesis for prior art before any compute is spent. Search the web for the proposed idea, its method name, and its central claim. If the idea already exists, say so and name the source — this is the cheapest point in the pipeline to catch it
- Distinguish a genuinely new idea from a restatement of known work in new vocabulary. Coining a term for an existing method is not originality, and should be scored as a major issue
- Judge ambition against what the request left OPEN. The less the request constrained, the more of that space the hypothesis was expected to claim; a safe, small study in answer to a wide-open question is a major issue, not a minor one
- Reject measurement dressed as contribution: an established measure, instrument or method applied to more cases — more models, languages, periods, countries, corpora or settings — is a table, not a finding. Say so plainly and ask for a claim that would change what someone in the field does or believes
- Ask whether the hypothesis is POSITIVE BY DESIGN — is there a mechanism that predicts the effect, or is the outcome a coin flip? If the direction is genuinely unknown, require that both outcomes be informative, or the run risks ending with an uninformative negative result

<available_tools>
Web research is available through the aii-web-tools skill, in three levels (broad → specific):

1. web search — Returns titles, URLs, snippets. Use first to discover and scan the landscape. Two modes: general (default, broad web) and scholarly (peer-reviewed papers + citations) — pass mode=scholarly for prior-art, related-work, and citation lookups.
2. web fetch — Reads a page and returns its content as markdown (HTML or PDF). Use to understand a source. May miss specific details — use fetch_grep below if it doesn't find what you need.
3. fetch_grep — Regex search over a page/PDF's full text. Returns exact matching sections with context. Use for precise details, exact numbers, methodology, or PDFs.

Workflow: search → fetch (understand) → fetch_grep (extract specifics).
</available_tools>

### [3] SYSTEM-USER prompt · 2026-08-20 19:27:55 UTC

````
<role>
You are a very experienced and critical conference reviewer specialized in the domain of the work under review.
You have reviewed for top-tier venues in the relevant field. Your reviews are known for
being thorough, fair, and grounded in the actual state of the field.
</role>

<hypothesis>
kind: hypothesis
title: Founder Fade Curve Predicts OSS Survival
hypothesis: >-
  An open-source project survives its founder stepping away if and only if the founder's involvement in the project followed
  a smooth, prolonged fade — a gradually declining trajectory, measurable as the slope of the founder's month-by-month share
  of merges, commits, and code-review decisions over the project's life — rather than an abrupt cliff or a flat plateau that
  ends suddenly. Specifically, the SHAPE of the founder's involvement trajectory over the entire pre-departure window predicts
  post-departure survival better than any static snapshot measure (bus factor, contributor count, project age, file count,
  popularity) at the moment of departure; projects whose founder's involvement fades gradually (a 'scaffolding fade') survive,
  while those whose founder maintained high involvement up to a sudden exit — or withdrew abruptly with no transition period
  — collapse.
motivation: >-
  Open-source software underpins global critical infrastructure, yet most OSS projects depend on one or two 'truck-factor'
  developers, and roughly half of those that lose their key people do not survive. Existing research measures this risk state-by-state
  — how many key developers there are (the bus factor) or what the size and bus factor of the project are at the moment of
  departure — and finds that simple counts explain little of the variation in survival. Decision-making authority (who actually
  merges, reviews, and decides) tends to concentrate in one founder, and management literature notes that a binary handover
  framing misses the empirically observed reality that most founders remain partially involved for an extended time. This
  hypothesis imports an established educational mechanism — Vygotsky/Bruner 'scaffolding with fading,' where a teacher gradually
  withdraws support as learners internalize the necessary skill — into OSS sustainability as a quantitative predictor: a slow
  founder fade signals that contributors are being scaffolded into caretaker capability, whereas a cliff leaves the contributor
  base unprepared. If confirmed, the finding would shift OSS risk assessment from snapshot metrics toward the temporal trajectory
  of decision-sharing, suggesting that maintainers should consciously decay their involvement as a survival practice and that
  ecosystem funders should evaluate trajectories rather than headcounts when triaging at-risk projects.
assumptions:
- >-
  The founder of an OSS project can be reliably identified from repository history as the user with the earliest sustained
  commit/merge activity (typically the repository creator), separable from other early 'key developers'.
- >-
  A project's survival after founder departure is a meaningful, labelable binary judgment — operationalized as continued sustained
  development activity (commits/merges above a threshold over the post-departure window) — analogous to Avelino et al.'s 'surviving
  system' criterion (attraction of at least one new truck-factor developer within the inactive period).
- >-
  The founder's involvement trajectory is meaningfully extractable from public repository artifacts: commit authorship, PR
  merges, and code-review participation, including the founder's SHARE of each over rolling time windows.
- >-
  The 'scaffolding fade' mechanism acts through capability transfer to contributors — gradual decline in the founder's share
  of merges/reviews gives contributors time to internalize decision-making skill — separable from passive 'bus factor' growth.
- >-
  A sufficient sample of public OSS projects with a founder who actually departs, with both survival and collapse outcomes,
  is obtainable from GitHub (on the order of tens to low hundreds of projects), fitting within the budget for cloning and
  parsing repository history.
investigation_approach: >-
  Assemble a curated cohort of public GitHub OSS projects whose identified founder has departed (defined by a 12-month inactivity
  window consistent with the abandonment-threshold literature). For each project, clone the repository locally and extract,
  per month or quarter from inception to founder departure, the founder's share of commits, share of PR merges, and share
  of code-review decisions; fit each trajectory to a small family of shape descriptors (linear slope, convexity, time-to-onset-of-decline,
  abrupt-cliff indicator, plateau-then-cliff indicator) and a quantitative 'fade index' (e.g., normalized integral over time).
  Independently label each project's survival using the Avelino et al. (2019) criterion — new active truck-factor developer(s)
  appearing after the founder's inactivity window with sustained subsequent activity — or, where labels are ambiguous, fall
  back on a continuous survival metric (e.g., normalized commit volume in the 24-month post-departure window relative to pre-departure
  baseline). Then build predictive models that compare (a) static baseline features (bus factor, contributor count, age, popularity,
  file count, computed at departure) against (b) the founder fade-curve shape descriptors, on both the binary survival label
  and the continuous post-departure activity retention. Use logistic regression and survival analysis (lifelines) for primary
  identification; complement with permutation-based feature importance and train/test splits. As a falsification control,
  run the same analysis using the fade curve of a randomly selected non-founder high-activity contributor in each project,
  expecting it to have lower predictive power than the founder fade curve if the mechanism is founder-specific.
success_criteria: >-
  Supporting evidence would be: (1) the founder-involvement fade descriptors significantly outperform static features (bus
  factor, contributor count, age, popularity) in cross-validated AUC/R-squared on predicting project survival after departure,
  with the fade-slope and abrupt-cliff indicator emerging as dominant feature importances; (2) a clear directional effect
  — projects whose founder involvement declined smoothly over time before departure are markedly more likely to survive than
  those whose founders maintained a flat plateau or ended with an abrupt cliff — with effect size stable across alternative
  inactivity thresholds and survival definitions; (3) the fade curve of the FOUNDER predicts survival better than the fade
  curve of a randomly chosen equally active non-founder contributor (founder-specific mechanism). Disconfirming evidence would
  be: fade descriptors add no measurable predictive value above static features, the directional effect reverses or is null
  after controlling for project age/popularity, or non-founder fade curves predict just as well (mechanism is generic, not
  founder-specific).
related_works:
- >-
  Avelino, Constantinou, Valente & Serebrenik (2019) 'On the abandonment and survival of open source projects: An empirical
  investigation' — Defines truck-factor-developer detachment (TFDD) and identifies surviving systems via the arrival of new
  truck-factor developers; comparisons use STATIC factors (number of developers/commits/files, project age) measured at the
  TFDD date and find weak separation. This hypothesis fundamentally differs by modeling the DYNAMIC TRAJECTORY of the FOUNDER's
  involvement over the whole pre-departure lifespan (gradual fade vs. abrupt cliff), not a static snapshot at the moment of
  detachment, and by singling out the founder rather than the anonymous set of key developers.
- >-
  Noori, Chakraborti, Zhang & Frey (2025) 'Patterns in the Transition From Founder-Leadership to Community Governance of Open
  Source' — Applies an NLP pipeline to GOVERNANCE.md files to document how textual governance (roles, actions, deontics) evolves
  across 637 repositories over time, characterizing institutional maturation. This hypothesis differs in MODALITY (it models
  the founder's behavioral trajectory in code/merge/decision artifacts rather than textual constitution artifacts), in OUTCOME
  (it predicts survival after departure rather than documenting governance evolution), and in MECHANISM (a specific measurable
  fade curve as a predictor, not documented policy growth).
- >-
  Kaushik & Chahal (2026) 'The Death Spiral of Open Source Projects: A Post-Mortem Analysis of Pull Request Workflow Dynamics'
  — Post-mortem analysis of aggregate PR workflow signals (friction, backlog growth, falling innovation, rising merge latency)
  across inactive vs. active GitHub projects, identifying a 'death spiral' and finding popularity/innovation are causes of
  survival while workflow friction is a byproduct. It models COMMUNITY-level aggregate dynamics AFTER decline begins and explicitly
  does not analyze the founder. This hypothesis models the FOUNDER-SPECIFIC involvement trajectory BEFORE departure and predicts
  survival-before-decline, a complementary mechanism not considered.
- >-
  Chen, Stol, Santos, German & Trinkenreich (ICSE 2026) 'How Does Core Contributor Disengagement Impact Open Source Project
  Activity? A Quasi-Experiment' — Uses Difference-in-Differences across 50,804 repositories to estimate the impact of core
  contributor disengagement on PR throughput/acceptance/merge time, finding impact varies with STATIC contributor profile
  (commit-share, tenure). This hypothesis differs by predicting whether the project SURVIVES the departure (not just its post-departure
  throughput decline), by focusing on the FOUNDER specifically, and crucially by using the dynamic trajectory of the founder's
  involvement across the whole project life as the predictor rather than a static profile at the moment of disengagement.
- >-
  Karim, Lu, Kasaadha & Goggins (2026) 'Predicting Open Source Software Sustainability with Deep Temporal Neural Hierarchical
  Architectures and Explainable AI' — Builds a hierarchical Transformer model over 24-month AGGREGATE activity sequences to
  classify OSS projects into lifecycle stages, with zero mention of founders or succession. This hypothesis differs by using
  a transparent, low-parameter fade-curve descriptor tied to ONE identified founder's involvement share across the project's
  full lifespan and targeting a distinct outcome (survival across an actual founder departure event).
- >-
  Vygotsky (1978) / Wood, Bruner & Ross (1976) scaffolding theory with fading — well-established educational mechanism: the
  more capable tutor provides structured support and gradually withdraws it (fading) so the learner internalizes and operates
  independently; sudden removal of support before competence matures causes collapse. The cross-domain transfer to OSS — treating
  the founder's involvement as scaffolding and predicting project post-departure survival from the shape of the fade — has
  not been previously operationalized in the OSS sustainability literature.
- >-
  Management literature on founder-CEO succession (e.g., Haveman et al. on organizational succession; Honjo & Kato 2022 on
  founder-CEO succession and firm survival; the observation that founder-CEOs often remain partially involved for some time)
  — studies binary outcomes of who succeeds and firm survival, not specifically the gradualness of involvement fade. The OSS
  adaptation here measures the actual trajectory shape continuously across behavioral artifacts, in a domain with public,
  fine-grained per-developer time series that firms do not expose.
inspiration: >-
  The Phase-1 divergence brainstorm covered eight cross-field directions (ecological succession, institutional path-dependence,
  tacit/explicit knowledge externalization, game-theoretic exit-threat credibility, structural-hole brokering, critical-slowing-down
  early warnings, allostatic load, and heterosis/cognitive diversity) — searches showed each was effectively present in OSS
  sustainability literature or recombination of existing approaches. The version that survived adversarial novelty checks
  imports the established educational mechanism of 'scaffolding with fading' (Vygotsky, Wood–Bruner–Ross) into OSS sustainability:
  just as a tutor must fade support gradually for a learner to internalize the skill, a founder must fade involvement gradually
  for the contributor community to take on decision-making; an abrupt withdrawal leaves the community unsupported and the
  project collapse-prone. The specific transfer is from the controlled-learning dynamic of tuition, where the role of 'more
  capable peer' has been formalized, to the distributed, unstructured OSS setting where no one has previously operationalized
  the founder's incremental-detachment trajectory as a survival predictor. Newer management-research observations (e.g., most
  founders stay partially involved for some time) hint at the empirical reality but in this OSS form the trajectory has not
  been measured or used as a predictor.
terms:
- term: Founder (OSS)
  definition: >-
    The principal early developer of the project, operationally defined for this study as the user with the earliest sustained
    commit/merge activity on the repository, typically the repository creator — distinguished from later key developers.
- term: Founder involvement trajectory
  definition: >-
    The time series of the founder's share of project activity per rolling time window (month or quarter) over the entire
    pre-departure lifespan, measured across multiple channels: commits authored, PR merges executed, and code-review decisions
    participated in.
- term: Fade curve / Scaffolding fade
  definition: >-
    The shape descriptor of the founder involvement trajectory — whether the founder's share declines smoothly over a sustained
    period ('scaffolding fade'), remains a flat plateau that ends abruptly ('plateau-then-cliff'), drops sharply ('abrupt
    cliff'), or oscillates — operationalized as slope, convexity, time-to-onset-of-decline, plateau-then-cliff indicator,
    and a composite fade index bounded between 0 (abrupt) and 1 (smooth fade).
- term: Scaffolding with fading
  definition: >-
    An educational-psychology principle (Vygotsky 1978; Wood, Bruner & Ross 1976) in which a more capable tutor provides structured
    support that is gradually withdrawn ('fading') as the learner internalizes the necessary skill; abrupt removal prior to
    competence causes collapse.
- term: Truck factor / Bus factor
  definition: >-
    The minimal number of developers whose simultaneous departure would seriously impair an OSS project (Williams & Cockwell;
    Cosentino et al.); a snapshot measure of how concentrated key project knowledge is.
- term: Truck-factor-developer detachment (TFDD)
  definition: >-
    Per Avelino et al. (2019), the event in which all truck-factor developers of a project become inactive within a defined
    abandonment window; a 'surviving system' is one where active new truck-factor developers subsequently appear.
- term: Survival after founder departure
  definition: >-
    A label for whether the project continues sustained development activity (commits/merges above a baseline threshold) over
    a defined post-departure window; operationally continued activity indicates survival, fall to inactivity indicates collapse.
summary: >-
  Whether an OSS project survives its founder stepping away is determined not by how many people there are at the moment of
  departure, but by the SHAPE of how the founder's involvement evolved across the project's whole life — smooth gradual decline
  ('scaffolding fade') signals a community prepared to take over and predicts survival; an abrupt cliff or unbroken plateau-then-cliff
  predicts collapse.
</hypothesis>

Quantifying the Impact of Core Developers\u2019 Dropout on\nthe Productivity of OSS Projects\nGiuseppe Russo Latona\nEPFL\ngiuseppe.russo@epfl.ch\nChristoph Gote\nETH Zurich\ncgote@ethz.ch\nChristian Zingg\nETH Zurich\nczingg@ethz.ch\nGiona Casiraghi\nETH Zurich\ngcasiraghi@ethz.ch\nLuca Verginer\nETH Zurich\nlverginer@ethz.ch\nFrank Schweitzer\nETH Zurich\nfschweitzer@ethz.ch\nABSTRACT\nOpen Source Software (OSS) projects play a critical role in the\ndigital infrastructure of companies and services provided to millions\nof people. Given their importance, understanding the resilience\nof OSS projects is paramount. A primary reason for OSS project\nfailure is the shock caused by the dropout of a core developer,\nwhich can jeopardize productivity and project survival. Using a\ndifference-in-differences (DiD) analysis, this study investigates the\nrepercussions of this shock on the productivity of 8,234 developers\nidentified among 9,573 OSS GitHub projects. Our findings reveal\nthe indirect impact of the core developer\u2019s dropout. The remaining\ndevelopers experienced a 20% productivity drop. This observation\nis troubling because it suggests that the shock might push other\ndevelopers to drop out, putting the collaboration structure of the\nproject at risk. Also, projects with higher productivity before the\nshock experienced a larger drop-down after the shock. This points\nto a tradeoff between productivity and resilience, i.e., the ability\nof OSS projects to recover from the dropout of a core developer.\nOur findings underscore the importance of a balanced approach\nin OSS project management, harmonizing productivity goals with\nresilience considerations.\nCCS CONCEPTS\n\u2022 Software and its engineering \u2192Software creation and man-\nagement; Collaboration in software development; \u2022 Human-\ncentered computing \u2192Empirical studies in collaborative and\nsocial computing.\nKEYWORDS\nOnline Collaboration Networks, Resilience, Causal Inference\nACM Reference Format:\nGiuseppe Russo Latona, Christoph Gote, Christian Zingg, Giona Casiraghi,\nLuca Verginer, and Frank Schweitzer. 2024. Shock! Quantifying the Impact\nof Core Developers\u2019 Dropout on the Productivity of OSS Projects. Copyrights for components of this work owned by others than the\nauthor(s) must be honored. Abstracting with credit is permitted. To copy otherwise, or\nrepublish, to post on servers or to redistribute to lists, requires prior specific permission\nand/or a fee. Request permissions from permissions@acm.org.\nWWW '24 Companion, May 13\u201317, 2024, Singapore, Singapore\n\u00a9 2024 Copyright held by the owner/author(s). Publication rights licensed to ACM.\nACM ISBN 979-8-4007-0172-6/24/05\nhttps://doi.org/10.1145/3589335.3651559\nMay 13\u201317, 2024, Singapore, Singapore. ACM, New York, NY, USA, 4 pages.\nhttps://doi.org/10.1145/3589335.3651559\n1\nINTRODUCTION\nEstimated 60% of global websites rely on Open Source Software\n(OSS) [9]. A Forbes survey of the Global Fortune 2000 companies [2]\nfound that 30% of these companies consider OSS projects \u2019extremely\ncritical\u2019 to their team\u2019s success. These two numbers underscore the\nimportance of the stability and performance of these projects key for\nthe digital ecosystem. However, OSS projects frequently face shocks\n[3], which may jeopardize the projects\u2019 security, functionality, and\noverall performance and eventually cause them to fail.\nIn this work, we study how the dropout of a core developer,\nhenceforth referenced as a shock, effects the OSS project. Specif-\nically, we address two research questions: (RQ1): How does the\ndropout of a core developer impact the productivity of the remain-\ning developers? (RQ2): Are projects with higher initial productivity\nmore resilient to such shocks? To answer these research questions,\nwe need to solve three methodological problems: (i) to identify\nOSS projects suffering from the specified shock, (ii) to compare\nthe productivity of projects before and after the shock, and (iii) to\nensure that observed changes in productivity can indeed be attrib-\nuted to the shock. To do so, we rely on causal inference techniques,\nspecifically on a two-stage matching process and a Difference-in-\nDifference analysis.\nFindings. We find that the dropout of core developers reduces\nthe project\u2019s productivity two-fold: First, because of the missing\ncontributions of the core developer, and second because of the\nadditional impact on the remaining developers. Our analysis reveals\na decline in productivity of the remaining developers of up to 20%\ncompared to those in non-exposed projects (RQ1).\nThis diminished productivity, persisting for at least 16 weeks,\nindicates that projects indeed had to struggle to recover from the\nshock. This highlights the importance of project resilience, defined\nas its ability to withstand and recover from shocks [19]. Our analysis\nreveals that small, but highly productive projects experience a more\nsignificant reduction in productivity after the shock. Hence, we\nobserve a tradeoff between productivity and resilience: Highly\nproductive projects are less resilient because their productivity\nincurs a larger drop-down in case of a shock (RQ2).\n2\nRELATED WORK\nWhy OSS developers leave projects. There are various reasons\nwhy developers leave OSS projects. They have found a new job or\n706\n\nTheWebConf, May 13\u201317, 2024, Singapore, Singapore\nGiuseppe Russo Latona et al.\nlost interest in the project [11], or become involved in a different\nproject [1]. Most OSS projects consist of a small number of core\ndevelopers and a large number of peripheral developers. While\nturnover among peripheral developers is common, the loss of a\ncore developer can significantly harm the project because of the\nloss of knowledge [10]. As a result, Coelho and Valente [3] found\nthat over 30% of the studied OSS projects terminated after their\nmain developers left.\nThe resilience of OSS projects. The question of whether\nOSS projects are capable of recovering from the loss of a core\ndeveloper is nowadays addressed using the term resilience. Various\nfactors have been identified to impact the resilience of OSS projects\n[3], including contributor motivation, project sustainability, and\ncommunity management strategies. Crowston and Howison [5]\nhave explored the social structure of OSS projects, highlighting\nthe importance of collaboration and communication for project\nsuccess. Similarly, Herbsleb [7] examined the role of turnover in\nOSS projects and emphasized the resulting challenges. Our work\nextends this line of research by explicitly focusing on the shock\non the projects\u2019 productivity after the sudden dropout of a core\ndeveloper.\n3\nDATA\nGitHub Data. We utilize GHTorrent [6], to collect over 125 mil-\nlion repositories from GitHub with their commits, developers, and\nco-editing information. As most of these projects are not collab-\norative (e.g., 67% have a single contributor or 56% of the projects\non GitHub are forks of original repositories), we discard projects\nwith the following criteria detailed by Kalliamvakou et al. [8]: (1)\nrepositories with only one developer, (2) repositories with fewer\nthan fifty commits, (3) repositories whose duration between the\noldest and newest commit is less than 100 days, and (4) repositories\nthat were forked from pre-existing projects. After the filtering, we\nare left with 4.5 million projects.\nAs we study the impact of a core developer dropping out, we\nseek OSS projects with one particularly active developer. To identify\nthese projects, we compute for each of the 4.5 million projects (1) the\nnormalized skewness of the commits distribution per developer, (2)\nthe project duration, and (3) the activity period of the core developer\nin the project. Following this procedure, we identify 9,573 projects\nwith an extremely active developer (e.g. skewness higher than 0.8).\nTreatment and Control. Our study aims to understand how\nthe dropout of the core developer from an OSS project impacts\nthe productivity of remaining developers in the same project. To\nquantify this impact, we compare the productivity of developers\nin OSS projects where the core developer left (treatment group) to\nthe productivity of developers in projects that were not exposed to\nsuch a shock (control group).\nThe projects for the treatment group are identified by setting a\nthreshold for the time lapsed after the core developer\u2019s last commit.\nTo determine this threshold, we adopt the method by Scholtes et al.\n[18] who demonstrated that after 42 weeks without a commit by\na developer, the probability of further contributions to the project\ndrops to 10%.\nThus, we establish a minimum time threshold of 42 weeks (294\ndays) between the core developer\u2019s last commit and our analysis\nCovariates\nProject Size\n(1) Number of developers: those that made one com-\nmit in the project. (2) Team size: number of develop-\ners that edited other developers\u2019 code. (3) Number\nof co-editing interactions.\nCollaboration Structure\n(1) Diameter of the collaboration network: it mea-\nsures the ease of collaboration between develop-\ners on a project. (2) Density of the network: inten-\nsity of the collaboration between developers. (3)\nClustering Coefficient: characterize the presence of\nsub-teams. (4) Mean Foreign Modification Ratio: ten-\ndency of developers to edit each others\u2019 code.\nWork Overload\n(1) Commits Skewness: quantifies the distribution\nof commits among developers to determine if the\nworkload disproportionately falls on specific indi-\nviduals. (2) Harmonic Centralization: Dependence\nof a project on a few central contributors.\nDevelopers\u2019 Covariates\nEgo Network\n(1) the Number of Distinct Connections: it measures\nthe size of a developer\u2019s collaboration neighbor-\nhood. (2) The Sum of Edge Weights: it quantifies the\ncollaboration intensity of a developer. (3) Number\nof Ego Components: it measures how much each\nindividual bridges different groups of the network.\nExternal Effort Activity\n(1) Focus Score: A metric to assess a developer\u2019s ef-\nfort distribution across projects. It is the weighted\naverage of the cosine similarity between the em-\nbeddings of the project associated with a developer\nand other projects where the developer contributed,\nwith weights being the number of commits in each\nproject [12].\nTable 1: Covariates used in the two-stage matching\ndate. We identified 101 projects where the core developers left with\n8,324 treated developers. Each developer in this treatment group is\nassociated with a single project.\nProductivity Outcome. To measure developer productivity,\nwe use Halstead\u2019s effort metric. It proxies the effort required to\nwrite a program, based on the number of operators and operands.\nSpecifically, if \ud835\udc411 is the total number and \ud835\udc5b1 the distinct number of\noperators, while \ud835\udc412 is the total number and\ud835\udc5b2 the distinct number of\noperands, the effort is calculated as the product of the program\u2019s dif-\nficulty (\ud835\udc37= \ud835\udc5b1\n2 \u00d7 \ud835\udc412\n\ud835\udc5b2 ) and its volume (\ud835\udc49= (\ud835\udc411+\ud835\udc412)\u00d7log2(\ud835\udc5b1+\ud835\udc5b2)).\nA decrease in this metric indicates more complex code, which is\npotentially harder to maintain and can also imply a higher likeli-\nhood of errors or bugs. Recent studies favor Halstead\u2019s effort as a\npreferred productivity metric among managers [3]. Additionally,\nwe use two productivity metrics complementary to Halstead\u2019s ef-\nfort: (1) Levenshtein distance of character edits in a commit and\n(2) Cyclometric complexity, reflecting the number of independent\npaths through the code. Because the results are similar to Halstead\u2019s\neffort, we focus on the latter.\n4\nMETHODS\nTo estimate the causal impact of a core developer\u2019s dropout on the\nproductivity of the remaining developers, we combine matching\nprocedures and difference-in-differences methods [17].\nTwo-Stage Matching. Following [15], we pair each developer\nfrom the treatment group with a developer from the control group.\nThis two-stage matching allows us to (1) account for selection bias\nand (2) balance the distribution of covariates of the treatment and\n707\n\nShock! Quantifying the Impact of Core Developers\u2019 Dropout on the Productivity of OSS Projects\nTheWebConf, May 13\u201317, 2024, Singapore, Singapore\ncontrol groups. For each project we calculate the propensity of\nexperiencing such a shock, P[\ud835\udc60\ud835\udc57= 1|\ud835\udc4b\ud835\udc56], given pre-event project\ncharacteristics \ud835\udc4b\ud835\udc57. A asi-Experiment\nYunqi Chen\nZhejiang University\nHangzhou, Zhejiang, China\nyunqichen@zju.edu.cn\nKlaas-Jan Stol\nLero and University College Cork\nCork, Ireland\nk.stol@ucc.ie\nFabio Santos\nColorado State University\nFort Collins, CO, USA\nfabio.deabreusantos@colostate.edu\nDaniel German\nUniversity of Victoria\nVictoria, BC, Canada\ndmg@uvic.ca\nBianca Trinkenreich\nColorado State University\nFort Collins, CO, USA\nbianca.trinkenreich@colostate.edu\nAbstract\nThe sustainability of Open Source Software (OSS) projects often\ndepends on a small group of core contributors. When these contrib-\nutors disengage, projects may face disruption; however, the con-\nsequences of such disengagement on OSS contribution workows\nremain underexplored. This paper presents a large-scale quasi-\nexperimental study quantifying the impact of core contributor\ndisengagement on pull request (PR) throughput, acceptance rate,\nand time to merge. We analyzed over 35 million PRs across 50,804\nGitHub repositories, identifying 95,958 disengagement bursts (one\nor more core contributors becoming inactive for >365 days within\nthe same week) involving 174,887 contributors. Using Dierence-in-\nDierences with matched control groups, we found that the impact\nvaries substantially with contributor and project characteristics.\nDisengagements of contributors with a high share of commits lead\nto pronounced declines in throughput and acceptance, while long-\ntenured contributors\u2019 disengagements have milder eects on those\nmetrics but increase merge time, suggesting loss of tacit project\nknowledge. These ndings provide empirical evidence of how core\ncontributors\u2019 disengagement inuences OSS workows and high-\nlight structural factors associated with project resilience.\nCCS Concepts\n\u2022 Software and its engineering ! Open source model.\nKeywords\nOpen source software, disengagement, attrition, productivity\nACM Reference Format:\nYunqi Chen, Klaas-Jan Stol, Fabio Santos, Daniel German, and Bianca\nTrinkenreich. 2026. How Does Core Contributor Disengagement Impact\nOpen Source Project Activity? A Quasi-Experiment. In 2026 IEEE/ACM 48th\nInternational Conference on Software Engineering (ICSE \u201926), April 12\u201318,\n2026, Rio de Janeiro, Brazil. ACM, New York, NY, USA, 14 pages. https:\n//doi.org/10.1145/3744916.3773135\nThis work is licensed under a Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International License.\nICSE \u201926, Rio de Janeiro, Brazil\n\u00a9 2026 Copyright held by the owner/author(s).\nACM ISBN 979-8-4007-2025-3/26/04\nhttps://doi.org/10.1145/3744916.3773135\n1\nIntroduction\nOpen Source Software (OSS) forms the backbone of today\u2019s digital\ninfrastructure, with an estimated 60% of websites relying on OSS\ntechnologies [40]. Despite their importance, OSS projects are highly\nvulnerable to the disengagement of key contributors, which can neg-\natively impact project sustainability [14]. The loss of contributors is\na major concern among OSS maintainers, project, and community\nmanagers, second only to poor code quality [51]. Core developer\nturnover has been shown to be a common and often unplanned phe-\nnomenon, even in mature ecosystems such as Rust, where over 60%\nof Rust modules (called \u2018crates\u2019) experienced at least one turnover\nevent over two years [24]. OSS project communities tend to fol-\nlow an \u2018onion\u2019 structure [16], with most contributors participating\neither sporadically or for short periods [4, 13], or with a limited\ncontribution rate. Only a limited number of core contributors are\ndeeply involved in the daily development process. This dependency\non a smaller number of core developers represents a major risk to\nOSS projects. Nearly two-thirds of 133 popular GitHub projects rely\non only one or two developers to survive [3]. In a follow-up study,\n16% of the sampled projects were abandoned by maintainers, and\n59% of those did not survive [2].\nNearly half of OSS project failures can be attributed to internal\nteam issues, such as contributor turnover and the loss of critical\nknowledge [14]. When core contributors\u2014sometimes referred to\nas \u2018truck factor\u2019 contributors due to their indispensable roles [87]\u2014\nleave a project, the impact can be severe. Over half of projects\nfail to recover from the departure of such contributors [2]. Core\ncontributor turnover has been associated with declines in team\nproductivity [55] and code quality [29, 72].\nAlthough studies have investigated abandonment [53], early\nsigns of disengagement from OSS projects [59], and the trade-o\nbetween productivity and resilience in the aftermath of these exits\n[66], there is a lack of understanding regarding the prevalence\nof core disengagement and its consequences for project activities.\nDespite being a common event, there is limited awareness of the\npotential long-term eects of disengagement. This highlights the\nneed for systematic investigations into how such disengagements\naect project-level collaboration and activity. Thus, we pose the\nfollowing two research questions:\nRQ1 What is the prevalence of core contributor disengage-\nment?\n\nHow Does Core Contributor Disengagement Impact Open Source Project Activity? A asi-Experiment\nICSE \u201926, April 12\u201318, 2026, Rio de Janeiro, Brazil\nRQ2 What impact does disengagement of core contributors\nhave on remaining contributors\u2019 project activity?\nFurther, any consequences likely vary based on the prole of\ndisengaged contributors, and the projects they disengage from.\nThis paper presents the results of a quasi-experiment, involving\na sample of over 50,000 OSS projects, investigating the trends of\ndisengagement among core contributors over the past decade, as\nwell as the impact of core contributors\u2019 disengagement on the\nproject activities of remaining contributors. A replication package\nfor this study is available online [11].\nIn the remainder of this paper, we discuss related work (Sec. 2),\nour research design (Sec. 3), followed by the results to RQ1 (Sec. 4)\nand RQ2 (Sec. 5). After discussing threats to validity (Sec. 6), we\nconclude this paper with a discussion of the results (Sec. 7).\n2\nRelated Work\n2.1\nReasons for Contributor Turnover\nA wide variety of factors can contribute to disengagement of open\nsource contributors. For some, a reduced level of interest to continue\ncontributing can cause developers to disengage [41, 54]. For others,\nmajor life changes such as a change of job can cause contributors\nto disengage, particularly when the new role is incompatible with\ncontributing to OSS (due to a lack of time or otherwise) [39, 41,\n54, 70]. Other reasons include governance issues in an OSS project\n[41], frustration with uncivil and toxic behaviors [23, 52], a lack of\ninclusiveness experiences [22, 30, 60], or lack of peer support [54].\nSeveral studies have explored behavioral signals of upcoming\ndisengagement and found that early indicators, such as bursty\ncontributions, limited social interactions, and low integration, can\nforecast attrition [59, 89, 93].\n2.2\nConsequences of Contributor Turnover\nThe consequences of the disengagement of contributors, especially\nthe most experienced, can have a negative impact on the sustainabil-\nity of the project. One issue that has been explored by the literature\nis the so-called \u2018truck factor\u2019 [3, 15, 28, 63]. A high truck factor\nindicates a resilient project with well-distributed knowledge, while\na low truck factor signals fragility due to reliance on a few key\ncontributors, especially if no new developers join a project [2].\nA high turnover in OSS projects has negative consequences, in-\ncluding knowledge loss [43, 64], decreased software quality [29],\ndecreased productivity [66], and lower survival probability [43, 72].\nTurnover within software teams has also been associated with a\nsignicant increase in customer-reported defects due to loss of\nknowledge and experience [55], while also impacting project sur-\nvival [43, 72], as approximately 80% of open source projects fail\ndue to contributor turnover related issues [72]. Recent research\n[24] investigated the challenges of core developers\u2019 turnover in\nthe Rust package ecosystem, including delayed updates and confu-\nsion over responsibilities. Our work complements this line of work\nby quantitatively estimating the causal impact of core contributor\ndisengagement on project activity across multiple GitHub ecosys-\ntems, including metrics such as pull request (PR) throughput, PR\nacceptance rate, and PR time to merge.\nFew studies in the Software Engineering domain have been using\ncausal inference methods to assess the impact of core developer\ndisengagement on project productivity [66]. Although contributor\nbreaks have been noted [4, 7, 41], the phenomenon of a disengage-\nment burst, where multiple core contributors leave within a short\nperiod, has hitherto not been studied. This study goes beyond es-\ntimating an average eect to examine the heterogeneous impacts\non project PR workow activities across dierent core contributor\nand repository characteristics, thereby providing a more nuanced\nand actionable perspective on the problem of core contributor dis-\nengagement.\n3\nResearch Design\nIn this section we lay out our research design.\n3.1\nCausal Inference Framework\nIn order to understand how much of the change in project activity\nis due to developer disengagement, we require a causal inference\napproach. This allows us to draw conclusions on cause and ef-\nfect, rather than only establishing associations between variables.\nSpecically, to empirically estimate the causal impact of core con-\ntributor disengagement bursts on GitHub project activities, we use\ndierence-in-dierences (DID) models [88] to investigate the eects\nof disengagement on project activities. DID models are widely used\nin econometrics and quantitative research in the social sciences\n[1, 9, 42, 65] and increasingly adopted in software engineering re-\nsearch [10, 25, 50, 66]. DID is a technique to look at the \u2018eect\u2019 of\nan \u2018intervention\u2019 from observational study data to mimic an ex-\nperimental research design. It considers a \u2018treatment\u2019 group and a\n\u2018control\u2019 group.1 For both, changes in mean values are calculated.\nIn this study, we considered those projects that suered from core\ncontributors disengaging as the \u2018treatment\u2019 group, and compared\nchanges in activity against projects that did not suer from disen-\ngaged core contributors (the \u2018control\u2019 group). A key requirement\nfor DID to be valid is the parallel trends assumption: if the disen-\ngagement had not occurred, the treatment group\u2019s activity would\nhave followed a similar trend as the control group. Although we\ncannot directly test this counterfactual situation, we carefully se-\nlected control groups based on pre-treatment observable trends to\nensure they share similar trends with the treatment group before\nthe disengagement burst.\nThe DID technique is usually applied to a single event (treat-\nment) at one point in time. Since disengagement events happen at\ndierent times across repositories in the real world, we adopt a DID\ndesign with what is called variations in treatment timing [35], to\n\u2018align\u2019 these events to a reference point. With such alignment, we\ncan use the regression analysis as a \u2018traditional\u2019 DID and estimate\nheterogeneous eects of contributor and project characteristics that\nmay moderate the impact of disengagement bursts (one or more\ncore contributors becoming inactive within the same week) [19].\n3.2\nData Collection and Preparation\nTo create the dataset for this study, we used SEART-GHS [18], which\nis a searchable dataset and tool that indexes GitHub projects based\non metadata and metrics. We applied the following ltering criteria.\nWe selected repositories that fullled the following criteria:\n1Because this was not a controlled experiment but rather a quasi-experiment (or\n\u2018natural\u2019 experiment), we quote the terms \u2018treatment,\u2019 \u2018control,\u2019 and \u2018intervention.\u2019\n\nICSE \u201926, April 12\u201318, 2026, Rio de Janeiro, Brazil\nYunqi Chen, Klaas-Jan Stol, Fabio Santos, Daniel German, and Bianca Trinkenreich\n\u2022 The project was created before November 2023, ensuring at\nleast one year of development history;\n\u2022 has at least 100 stars, indicating popularity;\n\u2022 is not a fork project, ensuring originality;\n\u2022 has a valid license, ensuring legal clarity for usage and con-\ntribution;\n\u2022 contains open issues, indicating active maintenance and com-\nmunity engagement;\n\u2022 has pull requests, reecting collaborative development;\n\u2022 includes contributions from at least 10 commit authors, demon-\nstrating team involvement;\n\u2022 has over 100 commits, indicating enough development activ-\nity.\nFrom this initial dataset, we selected projects using one of the 10\nmost popular programming languages [33] as their main language:\nPython, JavaScript, Java, TypeScript, C++, Golang, C#, C, PHP, and\nRust. Following prior work [94], we next excluded repositories\npotentially used for document storage or course submissions by\nltering out projects with keywords such as \u201chomework,\u201d \u201ccourse,\u201d\nand \u201cawesome\u201d in their names.\nFor each project, we used the GitHub API to collect its history\nof project activities (including commits and pull requests) until\nFebruary 2025. Projects whose repositories were deleted or made\nprivate during data collection were excluded, as their data could\nnot be fetched. We removed commits from bots as they were iden-\ntied by ltering login or username containing \u201cbot,\u201d followed by\nmanual validation. The internal socio-technical structure of projects has also\nbeen shown important, especially their dynamics. This points to\nanother angle on evaluating software success, from the perspective\nof self-sustaining and self-governing communities.\nTo uncover the dynamics of how a project at a nascent devel-\nopment stage gradually evolves into a sustainable one, here we\napply a socio-technical network modeling perspective to a dataset\nof Apache Software Foundation Incubator (ASFI), sustainability-\nlabeled projects. ACM, New York, NY, USA, 12 pages.\nhttps://doi.org/10.1145/3468264.3468563\n1\nINTRODUCTION\nOpen source has democratized software development. Developers\nflock to OSS projects hoping to add certain functionality, contribute\nto a worthy goal, and sharpen their skills. However, more than\n80% of OSS projects become abandoned over time, especially the\nsmaller and younger projects [44]. Certainly not all OSS projects\nare meant to be widely used or even to persist beyond a college\nsemester. However, even the large, popular OSS software, widely\nused in our daily lives and by fortune 500 companies, started out\nas small projects. Thus, from a societal perspective, it is important\nto ask: Why do some nascent OSS projects succeed and become\nself-sustaining while others do not [40]? And can the latter be\nhelped?\nTo aid developer communities to build and maintain sustainable\nOSS projects, nonprofit organizations like the Apache Software\nFoundation (ASF) have been established. ASF runs the ASF Incuba-\ntor (ASFI) [15], where nascent projects aiming to be a part of the\nASF community are provided with stewardship and mentor-like\nguidance to help them eventually become self-sustaining and even\ntop-level projects in ASF. Projects in ASFI, called podlings, are re-\nquired to adhere to ASF rules and regulations, including keeping all\ncommits and emails public. When certain conditions are satisfied,\nproject developers and ASF committees decide if a podling should\nbe graduated, referred to as a successful sustainability outcome. Oth-\nerwise they get retired. Per ASFI, \u2018A major criterion for graduation is\nto have developed an open and diverse meritocratic community. Grad-\nuation tests whether a project has learned enough and is responsible\nenough to sustain itself as such a community\u20191.\nPodlings in ASFI receive a mentor, file monthly reports, and\nget feedback. In spite of this support, many podlings fail. Most\nASFI committers do not lack coding expertise, but graduating from\nthe incubator requires more: it asks for effective teamwork and\nsustainable, community development. These requirements are most\nchallenging to meet. From comments in ASFI, we see that developers\nare confused by the expectation of \u2018The Apache Way\u2019, especially\ninitially. E.g., from project Flex on 05 Jan 2012, \u2018...I think there is\na need to keep things as simple as possible for people who [are]\n1https://incubator.apache.org/guides/graduation.html\n1056\n\nESEC/FSE \u201921, August 23\u201328, 2021, Athens, Greece\nLikang Yin, Zhuangzhi Chen, Qi Xuan, and Vladimir Filkov\nalready confused with what\u2019s going on with the move to Apache...\u2019\nThe frustrations sometimes persist beyond the initial period. A\ncomment from project Flex on 06 Jan 2012, states \u2018...many people\nare confused and lost as to what to do. Who is providing that\ndirection for them?\u2019 In large part, understanding how to achieve\nthe graduation requirements seems to be the culprit, likely due\nto the abstract nature of those concepts. Another reason is that\ncomparison to others is difficult. From project Rave on 28 June 2011:\n\u2018[sporadic adherence to requirements] makes it very confusing and\ndifficult to compare against what other projects as some are doing\ntoo little while others are doing too much...\u2019\nThus, there is a need to connect the proverbial dots on how to\nget from the point of entry into ASFI to checking all the graduation\nrequirement boxes. That brings us to the motivation of our paper.\nThe extrinsically labeled ASFI dataset offers heretofore unavail-\nable, fine-grained records of historical trajectories of projects under\npolicies and regulations of ASFI. We posit that:\nThe process that ASFI projects follow toward becoming sus-\ntainable can be modeled as a function of a small set of project\nfeatures, so that the outcome (graduation/retirement) can be\npredicted early on, from the successes and failures of others,\nallowing for trajectory adjustment if needed.\nTo deliver on that, in addition to the historical, outcome-labeled\nASFI data, we also need a theoretical framework that can capture\nthe complexity of OSS development. Over the past 30 years research\nin organizational behavior and management has documented the\nevolution in project management practices [8], as they have moved\ntoward more successful models [2]. The socio-technical view of\nan organization [30] has emerged as one of the more successful\nhybrid models recognizing the benefits that integrated treatment\nof the technical aspect (code, machines, device, etc.) and the social\naspect (people, communication, well-being, etc.) has on an organi-\nzation [36]. Likewise, OSS projects have been effectively studied\nfrom the socio-technical system (STS) perspective [1], with the social\nside capturing humans and their communication channels, and the\ntechnical capturing the content and structure of the software [43].\nHere, inspired by the socio-technical systems modeling perspec-\ntive, and the availability of extrinsically labeled historical data of\nproject sustainability from ASFI, our goals are: (1) to identify socio-\ntechnical features distinguishing projects that graduate from the\nASF Incubator from those that do not (i.e., find the determinants\nof OSS project sustainability), and (2) to build temporal forecast-\ning models that can predict sustainability outcomes at any time\npoint in the project development, and thus (3) to offer practical and\ntimely advice on intervening to correct a project\u2019s course, especially\nearly on. To approach these goals, we conduct a mix of quantitative\nand qualitative empirical studies. We start by gathering project\ntechnical traces (commits) and social traces (emails) from the ASF\nIncubator website 2. From those, we construct the temporal social\nand technical networks for each project, and perform exploratory\ndata analyses, deep-dive case studies, build an accurate forecasting\nmodel, and finally implement the interpretable model presenting\ntimely advice. We illustrate our workflow in Figure 1.\n2http://incubator.apache.org/projects/\nASFI\nMailing Lists\nASFI\nSVN Commits\nSocial Nets\nTechnical Nets\nLSTM-based\nModel\nQuantitative\nAnalysis\nQualitative\nAnalysis\nPart I:\nData Collection\nand Cleaning\nPart II:\nLongitudinal Socio-\ntechnical System\nPart III:\nDeep Learner &\nActionable Model\nPart IV:\nMixed Method\nAnalysis\nActionable\nModel\nFigure 1: The workflow of our mixed-methods study.\nOur contributions in this paper are:\n\u2022 We provide a novel longitudinal dataset of hundreds of OSS\nprojects\u2019 development traces under ASF regulation, with\nextrinsically labeled project sustainability status.\n\u2022 We propose the first OSS project sustainability forecast mea-\nsure modeled from tens of socio-technical network and project\nfeatures. Our model shows excellent predictive performance\n(\u226593% accuracy as early as 8 months into incubation).\n\u2022 We find that ASF incubator projects with fewer but more\nfocused committers and more but distributed (participating\nin asynchronous discussions) communicators are more likely\nto gain momentum to self-sustainability.\n\u2022 We describe a strategy for real-time monitoring of the sus-\ntainability forecast for any project, derived from an inter-\npretable version of our DNN model.\nThis paper is a first step toward showing that end-results of\nOSS projects\u2019 sustainability trajectories can be effectively forecast\nand possibly corrected upward, if needed. To minimize conflicts, ASF requires\nprojects to make all communication publicly available on the mail-\ning lists, summarized popularly as if it did not happen in the mailing\nlists, it did not happen [42]. For example, contributors are invited to vote +1 (yes), 0\n1057\n\nSustainability Forecasting for Apache Incubator Projects\nESEC/FSE \u201921, August 23\u201328, 2021, Athens, Greece\n(okay), or \u22121 (no) to project-wide changes. However, ASF commit-\nters can live in different time zones, and their response to a project\ndecision can delay largely. Regarding that, ASF community adopts\nthe Lazy Consensus [31]. Moreover, the ASF community also be-\nlieves in Earned Rights, that newcomers should be treated the same\nway if they have proven their technical skills.\nThe goal of the ASF Incubator (ASFI) is to help projects become\nself-sustained and eventually join ASF. {"content": [{"cache_prompt": false, "type": "text", "text": "URL: https://www.se.cs.uni-saarland.de/publications/docs/BAJ+23.pdf\nType: PDF\nLength: 117814 chars (truncated)\n\n--- Content ---\n\nAutomatic Core-Developer Identification on GitHub:\nA Validation Study\nTHOMAS BOCK, Saarland University, Saarland Informatics Campus, Germany\nNILS ALZNAUER, Saarland University, Saarland Informatics Campus, Germany\nMITCHELL JOBLIN, Siemens AG, and Saarland University, Saarland Informatics Campus, Germany\nSVEN APEL, Saarland University, Saarland Informatics Campus, Germany\nMany open-source software (OSS) projects are self-organized and do not maintain official lists with information on developer\nroles. So, knowing which developers take core and maintainer roles is, despite being relevant, often tacit knowledge. We propose\na method to automatically identify core developers based on role permissions of privileged events triggered in GitHub issues\nand pull requests. In an empirical study on 25 GitHub projects, (1) we validate the set of automatically identified core developers\nwith a sample of project-reported developer lists, and (2) we use our set of identified core developers to assess the accuracy of\nstate-of-the-art unsupervised developer classification methods. Our results indicate that the set of core developers, which\nwe extracted from privileged issue events, is sound and the accuracy of state-of-the-art unsupervised classification methods\ndepends mainly on the data source (commit data vs. issue data) rather than the network-construction method (directed vs.\nundirected, etc.). Methodol. , (April 2023), 28 pages. https://doi.org/10.1145/nnnnnnn.nnnnnnn\n1\nINTRODUCTION\nCoordination among software developers is critical to ensure software quality and to drive software evolution [20,\n44, 53, 55]. This holds especially for open-source software (OSS) projects, in which volunteers can participate and\ndevelopers are globally distributed, often not knowing each other personally [5, 43]. With increasing project size\nand popularity, developers who hold leadership roles (i.e., who take care of the project\u2019s health and are highly\ninvolved in its long-term maintenance) are crucial for a thriving evolution of the project [23, 61, 99, 102]. As\nopposed to closed-source software projects, where leadership positions are typically established by mandated\norganization structures and well-determined within the organization, OSS projects often do not provide explicit,\npublicly recognizable information regarding group structures and project responsibilities. So, despite the lack of\npredefined hierarchical group structures in OSS projects, certain developers take particular responsibilities in\nthe project, such as performing maintenance tasks or implementing core functionality. Such developers, often\ncalled maintainers or core developers [46, 48, 66, 68], become crucial to these projects, as they hold key knowledge\nabout source code and software architecture [78] and shape the character and culture of the project [1, 92, 108],\nespecially when OSS projects grow fast in terms of developers and amount of source code [36]. As a prominent\nexample, the Linux kernel maintains a public list of maintainers, who are responsible for specific subsystems\nand who have deep knowledge about the project [107]. Using this list, external and internal developers can\nfind out whom to contact for any matters. Unfortunately, many OSS projects do not maintain such a public,\ncurated list. Especially for newcomers in a software project, but also for developers that are not consistently\nactive (also called peripheral developers [27, 48, 66, 68]), it is important to know who is playing the role of a\ncore developer, who is making decisions and maintaining the code base, and who will finally accept or reject\ncode contributions [62, 84]. In well-organized projects, this might not be an actual problem for newcomers,\nas one can easily check who has recently reviewed or merged pull requests. Some projects even use bots to\nautomatically assign developers for reviewing a new pull request [94]. However, in projects that are badly\norganized and which do not use such well-structured code-review processes, it might be not that easy to find\nout whom to contact in the case of questions, for instance. Knowing how developers organize is also important\nfor companies, to decide whether and how to invest in a project or how to efficiently contribute to an OSS\nproject [16, 33, 106, 109]. For researchers, knowing about core developers also aids the investigation of a project\u2019s\nhierarchical structure, which helps avoid high developer turnover and identify organizational smells that could\nendanger project success [19, 24, 29, 42, 55, 75, 86].\nThere is a growing corpus of research that attempts to automatically extract information about the core\ndevelopers of a project. A state-of-the-art approach is to construct a developer network from various data sources\n(e.g., commits, e-mails, issue comments) and to apply network-based centrality metrics. The assumption is that core\ndevelopers are responsible for about 80% of the contributions and activities in the project [48, 49, 51, 62, 65, 101,\n105], attaining central positions within the network due to the high number of contributions and many interactions\nwith other developers. While this is an appealing approach, the actual performance of such classification methods\non the identification of core developers is largely unclear, especially as there are various ways on how to construct\ndeveloper networks and also many, possibly contradicting classification methods can be applied thereafter.\nTherefore, we devise a method to automatically derive a set of core developers and maintainers from privileged\nevents in GitHub issue discussions and pull requests. The rationale is that GitHub permits triggering certain\nevents (e.g., merging a pull request) only to user accounts that have been assigned to a certain role. Nevertheless,\nthese privileged events cannot be used to identify potential candidates for future core developers before they\nget any privileges in the project and also not for projects that user other social coding platforms than GitHub\nissues. Therefore, other classification methods for identifying core developers are still essential, which is why it\nis also our goal to assess the accuracy of the state-of-the-art classification methods. INTRODUCTION\nProducing software is a multifaceted activity that requires\nexpertise beyond just writing code. Besides developers, tech\norganizations employ workers in diverse roles such as testing,\nproduct management, human resources, sales, and so forth\n[1]. All of these roles contribute to the core product [2], if\nindirectly, and are recognized as such.\nUnlike industrial software, open source software (OSS) is\noften developed under a non-traditional structure and, as a\nresult, is seen as the product of teams composed almost\nexclusively of developers [3]. This picture is of course in-\ncomplete at best, as is well-known by those involved with\nOSS [4]. While young projects can thrive under the guidance\nof lone developers or small unsupported teams, more mature\nprojects usually bene\ufb01t from contributions to the project that\ntranscend code [4]. Someone who is well acquainted with a com-\nmunity might be highly aware of who spent a lot of time\nmoderating a community, developing the project\u2019s road map,\nor managing the queue of issues. However, this information is\nrarely recorded in a standardized way, particularly within soft-\nware repositories. Instead, OSS projects tend to acknowledge\ncontributions in ad-hoc ways, with mechanisms that include,\nfor example: \ufb02at credit \ufb01les, acknowledgments appearing on a\nproject\u2019s website, challenge coins handed out to contributing\ncommunity members [8], or even academic papers providing\ncitable artifacts and a snapshot of a project at a particular\nmoment in time. These models of acknowledgments are far\ntoo diverse and variable to mine at scale. Further, they are not\nalways attached to the software project itself. As a result, when\nmining software repositories, we are more or less reduced to\nde\ufb01ning contributorship in terms of changes to the repositories,\nwhether it be at the levels of lines of codes, commits, pull or\nmerge requests, or patches.\nDe\ufb01nitions of contributorship that emphasize code changes\nare far from perfect. First, strictly focusing on the data mining\nperspective, code as the measure of contribution tends to\nprovide an inaccurate picture of OSS projects. For example,\ntwo de\ufb01nitions can be seemingly in agreement\u2014say, having\ncommitted code or opened a pull request\u2014yet lead to different\nconclusions about how OSS is made [9]. Commit data may\nbe erased or altered during code merges and revisions [10],\nmaking decentralized version control logs unreliable for mea-\nsuring contributorships [11]. As we have already mentioned,\n242\n2021 IEEE/ACM 18th International Conference on Mining Software Repositories (MSR)\n978-1-7281-8710-5/21/$31.00 \u00a92021 IEEE\nDOI 10.1109/MSR52588.2021.00036\n\ncontributorship, de\ufb01ned as the changing of code, does not\ncover the complete spectrum of contributions [6, 12]. Not only do developers routinely con-\ntribute code without feeling a sense of ownership or deeming\nthemselves contributor to a project [4, 13], but increasingly\nlarge parts of code changes are implemented by bots that act\nas users [14]. Finally, there are pronounced gender [15\u201317]\nand geographical [18] differences in the way people contribute\nto OSS\u2014such that \u201ccommit as contribution\u201d is not likely to\ncapture a representative cross-section of contributors.\nNow, given that: (1) \u201ccommit as contribution\u201d is a \ufb02awed\nmodel, and (2) more representative acknowledgment models\nare hard to mine due to a lack of standardization across\nprojects, one might wonder: Are we destined to mischarac-\nterize contributorship to OSS projects in all our large-scale\nanalyses?\nFortunately, new tools [19], and models [1, 20] could help\nus derive a more accurate picture of who makes OSS and\nhow. These tools and models aim to provide more systematic\nand complete coverage of the types of contributions routinely\nmade to OSS. For example, tools like octohatrack [21]\nand name-your-contributors [22] can generate lists\nof all the users that have interacted with a GitHub project,\nincluding those who opened issues, created pull requests, or\ndiscussed issues. Models like the All Contributors (AC) project\naim to standardize credit \ufb01les [20], and recommend that all the\ncontributors be credited together with the types of contribution\nthey have made, such as coding, code review, translation\nwork, or \ufb01nancial support. We organize our analysis around a series\nof research questions. First, we ask:\n\u2022 RQ1: How do models of contributorship acknowledgment\ndiffer?\n\u2022 RQ2: How much information is missed by focusing on\nrepository changes as the model of contribution?\nHaving understood the kind of information given by various\nsystems of contributions acknowledgment, we then focus on:\n\u2022 RQ3: How do contribution to open source projects evolve\nas they age?\n\u2022 RQ4: Can we classify projects based on patterns of\ncontributions?\nWe discuss our answers to these questions in detail in\nSec. IV and V, but we highlight three key \ufb01ndings here:\n1) Generosity. Models of contribution acknowledgment\nthat require manual attribution of credits recognize fewer\ncontributors than other models but that they can cover\nwork not captured by other methods (even by proxy).\n2) Large blind spots. This is critical evidence. Both papers establish that core-developer and founder identification from repository artifacts are acknowledged as inaccurate, with documented failure modes: identity aliases, bots, "commit-as-contribution" biases, code authored but attributed to someone else during merges (commit co-authorship, git history rewrites). The "Automatic Core-Developer Identification" TOSEM paper shows that classification accuracy depends heavily on data source (commits vs. issues) and that no unsupervised method produces a "reliable ground-truth."

The hypothesis assumes "the repository creator" = the founder = "earliest sustained commit activity." But the literature shows commit attribution is fragile and an "earliest committer" heuristic ignores known cases (e.g., projects imported/squashed-rebased into a new repo, founders who initially hosted on GitLab/Hg/SVN and migrated, founders whose work was coauthored, founders who stepped away then returned). This is directly the methodological concern.

### [66] ASSISTANT · 2026-08-20 19:34:42 UTC

```
Two important confirmations:
1. **Avelino et al. confirms**: 315 projects abandoned (16%), only 128 survived (41%). The paper does detailed descriptive statistics + survey but does NOT explicitly claim static factors "explain little of the variation in survival" with a prediction model. The hypothesis characterizes Avelino as finding "weak separation" — partially true (descriptive differences), but the framing slightly overstates — Avelino never explicitly compares vs. trajectory features. This is a defensible reading, but the "explains little" claim is the hypothesis author's interpretation, not Avelino's explicit finding. This is a minor framing concern.
2. **Honjo & Kato citation**: There IS a real Honjo & Kato working paper on "CEO succession and new-firm performance: Does CEO origin matter?" — but it's dated around 2020 (Kwansei Gakuin / Chuo University working paper), NOT "Honjo & Kato 2022." The closest match is a 2020 abstract; there is no 2022 journal publication. So the citation year appears to be inaccurate/misattributed — at best loosely grounded (there's a real Honjo–Kato research line) but the specific "2022" claim is not verified. Minor citation-hygiene issue.

Now let me check the most critical methodological question for the hypothesis: does the proposal to *train predictive models on 10s–100s of samples* with feature comparison have enough power? And does the "founder inactivity window" create a label self-fulfilling-prophecy problem (if you define departure as 12 months inactive AND define survival as post-window activity, the labels could leak)? Let me also check the most directly related "trajectory before departure" work — the "Predicting contributor turnover from value-related discussions" (Jamieson ICSE 2024) and "Why do People Give Up FLOSSing" (Miller et al. disengagement) to confirm they model post or concurrent, not pre-departure founder trajectory as scaffold-predictor.
```
