A Study of\nContributor Disengagement in Open Source\nCourtney Miller1\u22c6, David Widder2, Christian K\u00a8astner2, and Bogdan Vasilescu2\n1 New College of Florida, USA\n2 Carnegie Mellon University, USA\nAbstract. Established contributors are the backbone of many free/libre\nopen source software (FLOSS) projects. Previous research has shown\nthat it is critically important for projects to retain contributors and it\nhas also revealed the motivations behind why contributors choose to\nparticipate in FLOSS in the \ufb01rst place. However, there has been lim-\nited research done on the reasons why established contributors disen-\ngage, and factors (on an individual and project level) that predict their\ndisengagement. In this paper, we conduct a mixed-methods empirical\nstudy, combining surveys and survival modeling, to identify the reasons\nand predictive factors behind established contributor disengagement. We\n\ufb01nd that di\ufb00erent groups of established contributors tend to disengage\nfor di\ufb00erent reasons, however, overall contributors most commonly cite\nsome kind of transition (e.g., switching jobs or leaving academia). We\nalso \ufb01nd that factors such as the popularity of the projects a contributor\nworks on, whether they have experienced a transition, when they work,\nand how much they work are all factors that can be used to predict their\ndisengagement from open source.\n1\nIntroduction\nContributor disengagement in open source is widely known as a costly and criti-\ncal issue [9, 20, 54], as it can directly a\ufb00ect the sustainability of projects; e.g., in a\nrecent study Coelho et al. reported that 41 % of failed open source projects cited\na reason involving the developer team, such as lack of interest or time of the\nmain contributor [10]. Such local (project-level) sustainability issues in open-\nsource can have cascading e\ufb00ects on the entire ecosystem because of project\ninterdependencies [13, 59]. So-called \u201ccore\u201d, i.e., established, contributors are\nparticularly critical for the sustainability of open-source projects [20, 64].\nThere are many reasons why established contributors disengage. Some may\nbe unavoidable, whereas others could perhaps be prevented through interven-\ntions or by providing better community support. Likely there are various dy-\nnamics in play, including the role of volunteers as compared to corporate em-\nployees [47], the role of external events such as family planning and job changes,\nand the role of perceived purpose, community support, and stress. E\ufb00ects might\ninclude abruptly leaving the project, but also slow disengagement, or causing\nrippling frustrations through delays or cynicism.\n\u22c6Part of this work was carried our during the author\u2019s REU program at CMU.\n\n2\nC. Miller et al.\nThe goal of our research is to better understand disengagement factors and\nwhich established contributors are at risk and when; this will enable us to build\nand validate a conceptual framework and theory. Moreover, we pursue a data-\ndriven approach, operationalizing uncovered factors based on publicly available\ntrace data. This way, we can identify at-risk open-source contributors and com-\nmunities, and help guide resources (e.g., volunteers, sponsors) toward projects\nand contributors in need, enhancing the sustainability of the overall ecosystem.\nWe identify potential disengagement factors from literature on turnover and\nopen-source retention, cross-validate them with results from a survey among con-\ntributors who recently stopped all open-source activities on GitHub, operational-\nize select factors with public trace data, and \ufb01nally conduct survival modeling\namong a set of 206 GitHub users to triangulate the survey results.\nAmong others, we identify the degree to which contributors work outside\nof typical o\ufb03ce hours and to what degree they engage in support activities as\nimportant moderating factors. According to Claes et al. [8], 33 % of open-source\ncontributors do not follow typical working hours, but instead work nights and\nweekends. Our survey shows that contributors who work nights and weekends\nproportionally tend to disengage for di\ufb00erent reasons than those working regular\nhours. In addition, our survey reveals that the most common reasons for complete\ndisengagement relate to transitions in employment, such as graduating from\nacademia, changing employers, and changing roles.\nTo validate disengagement factors beyond our survey, we model to what de-\ngree hypothesized factors\u2014such as working hours, engagement in support activ-\nities, and team size, which can be measured in public trace data of contributor\nactivities\u2014can predict the later disengagement of those contributors. To that\nend, we use the quantitative statistical method of survival modeling. As a key\nfactor in our model, derived from our survey results, we incorporate transitions\nidenti\ufb01ed from public CVs of developers. Speci\ufb01cally, we analyze which contrib-\nutor populations are more resilient to transitions such as job changes.\nWe \ufb01nd that working predominantly during o\ufb03ce hours and experiencing\na transition both increase a contributors risk of disengagement. Conversely, we\n\ufb01nd that increased levels of activity and working on more popular projects both\ndecrease a contributors risk of disengagement.\nIn summary, we contribute (1) a survey revealing the reasons behind contrib-\nutor disengagement; (2) a comparison between di\ufb00erent groups of contributors;\n(3) measures to di\ufb00erentiate between groups, which could be used to help identify\nat-risk groups and better target support interventions; (4) a novel operational-\nization of transition data; and (5) a survival model demonstrating which factors\nare able to predict contributor disengagement.\n2\nRelated work\nTurnover. Prior work has shown that the turnover rate of a project profoundly\na\ufb00ects its survival probability [35, 50] and code quality [23]. Approximately 80 %\nof open source projects fail due to contributor turnover related issues [50]. Even\n\nWhy do People Give Up FLOSSing?\n3\nwithin projects that do not outright fail, contributor turnover has a signi\ufb01cant\nadverse e\ufb00ect on software quality [23]. On a project level, contributor disengage-\nment results in knowledge loss, which is a particularly expensive issue [35].\nEmployee turnover and retention have been broadly studied across many\n\ufb01elds [33, 37]. In professional settings, early turnover research has focused often\non personal characteristics (e.g., ability, age) and employee satisfaction, mea-\nsured with hiring tests and surveys, whereas later research has explored many\nmore nuanced factors, such as labor market (e.g., job opportunities), non-work\nvalues, and organizational commitment [33]. Research has shown that, while far\nfrom all turnover can be explained by dissatisfaction and similar factors [40],\nthere are positive and negative factors that can bu\ufb00er against shocks such as\nexternal job o\ufb00ers [6, 22]. Turnover among volunteers is less explored: Although\nsome research suggests that similar personal and environmental factors in\ufb02u-\nence their decisions to quit [44], other researchers point out that satisfaction\nand achievement, compatible working hours, training, challenging work, and role\nidentity may play particularly strong roles [27, 36, 56].\nWhereas reasons for joining open source [5, 26, 39, 48, 53, 61, 62] and inter-\nventions to improve the onboarding experience for new developers [7, 19, 32, 58]\nhave been studied in depth, studies of contributor retention are rarer. Prior\nresearch [12, 41, 43, 51, 55, 60, 65] tested mostly basic attributes. For exam-\nples, they have shown that retention is higher for contributors that have partici-\npated longer [41, 55], contributed more code changes [12, 41], and communicated\nmore [12]. Using surveys, researchers further associated ratings of general dis-\nsatisfaction and lack of community identi\ufb01cation with higher perceived turnover\nand turnover intentions [34, 63]. Zhou et al.\u2019s case study of three projects further\nsuggests that commercial participation can crowd out volunteers [65].\nLong working hours, lack of sleep, and lack of recovery on weekends are\noften discussed as stressors. Many studies con\ufb01rm the importance of \u201cmentally\nswitching o\ufb00\u201d [1, 4, 57]. In software engineering, several studies have shown the\nin\ufb02uence of time-related factors, such as late-night commits and long working\nsessions being more likely to contain bugs [18, 49], sleep deprivation reducing\ncode quality [24], Monday commit comments using more negative language [31],\nand time pressure is often seen as an important stressor [38].\nOpen-source practitioners reporting stress. ### [71] ASSISTANT · 2026-08-20 19:34:56 UTC

```
Both papers confirm:
- The **Miller et al. FLOSSing study**: Interview-based, small sample (206 users), models the *who/why disengages* but uses *static + survival factors* (working hours, popularity, transitions), NOT a per-contributor involvement trajectory shape predicting project survival. Distinct from the hypothesis, and confirms that "abruptly leaving" vs "slow disengagement" IS known as a phenomenon, but never operationalized as a predictor of *project survival*.
- **Jamieson ICSE 2024**: Predicts project *turnover* (in/out contributing) from value discussions via Granger causality on 52 projects — a *temporal leading-indicator* approach, but for turnover flows not founder-specific fade or project survival.

This strengthens the **novelty assessment**: the specific operationalization (founder's *pre-departure share trajectory shape* → *project survival*) does NOT appear to exist in the literature. The closest precedents (Chen et al. disengagement impact, Shock! dropout, Avelino TFDD survival) all use *static-at-the-moment* features for impact, or *post-departure* dynamics.

Now I have enough evidence to assess the genuinely important issues. Let me do a final check on whether the "gradual withdrawal = better survival" mechanism is strongly predicted by the scaffolding analogy or whether there are competing confounds, and whether the "plateau-then-cliff" confounds with project type. Let me also do a quick search to see if there's any *specific empirical pattern* finding gradual declines predict survival — the strongest potential prior-art.
```

Resilience in Free/Libre/Open Source Software: Do founder decisions impact development activity after crisis events?\n\n\n\n\n# Resilience in Free/Libre/Open Source Software: Do founder decisions impact development activity after crisis events?\n\n## Files\n\n[ Hale_washington_0250O_22529.pdf  (258.01 KB)](/researchworks/bitstreams/186f14b1-3a26-47b6-84a7-ef472f4dce04/download \"Download Hale_washington_0250O_22529.pdf\")\n\n## Date\n\n[2021-07-07](/researchworks/browse/dateissued?startsWith=2021-07-07)\n\n## relationships.isAuthorOf\n\nHale, Wm Salt\n\n## Journal Title\n\n## Journal ISSN\n\n## Volume Title\n\n## Publisher\n\n## Abstract\n\nFree/Libre/Open Source Software (FLOSS) is perhaps the most studied of the information-based communal public goods. In this piece, I conceptualize development resilience and founder decisions as two important characteristics of FLOSS. By asking whether the impact of the latter can be measured through the former, I outline three hypotheses concerning software licensing, crisis events, and the interaction between the two. I then conduct a time series analysis of a novel dataset containing longitudinal measures of software packages from the Debian Project. I determine that while the development activity of all projects declines over time, this decline is slower for protectively licensed projects than permissively licensed ones. I also find that the development activity of FLOSS projects increases after encountering a Debian Security Advisory. Combining these predictors, I find that permissively licensed projects demonstrate more development resilience than protectively licensed ones.\n\n## Description\n\nThesis (Master's)--University of Washington, 2021\n\n## Keywords\n\n[commons-based peer production](/researchworks/browse/srsc?startsWith=commons-based%20peer%20production), [computational social science](/researchworks/browse/srsc?startsWith=computational%20social%20science), [free/libre/open source software (FLOSS)](/researchworks/browse/srsc?startsWith=free%2Flibre%2Fopen%20source%20software%20\\(FLOSS\\)), [organizational founders](/researchworks/browse/srsc?startsWith=organizational%20founders), [software development](/researchworks/browse/srsc?startsWith=software%20development), [software licensing](/researchworks/browse/srsc?startsWith=software%20licensing), [Communication](/researchworks/browse/srsc?startsWith=Communication), [Social research](/researchworks/browse/srsc?startsWith=Social%20research), [Intellectual property](/researchworks/browse/srsc?startsWith=Intellectual%20property)\n\n## Citation\n\n## URI\n\n[ http://hdl.handle.net/1773/47038](http://hdl.handle.net/1773/47038)\n\n## DOI\n\n## Collections\n\n[Communications](/researchworks/collections/ea888303-9d7c-4d0c-bbe1-e742a0f2d779)\n\n[ __Full item page](/researchworks/items/db2cc94b-a53f-4a5d-ad17-69ad86e8711f/full)\n\n[DSpace software](http://www.dspace.org/) copyright \u00a9 2002-2026 [LYRASIS](https://www.lyrasis.org/)\n\n  * Cookie settings\n  * [UW Scholarly Publishing Services](https://lib.uw.edu/research-services/publishing/)\n  * [Accessibility settings](/researchworks/info/accessibility)\n  * [Privacy policy](/researchworks/info/privacy)\n  * [End User Agreement](/researchworks/info/end-user-agreement)\n  * [Send Feedback](/researchworks/info/feedback)"}], "is_error": false, "command": "export SKILL_DIR=\"/ai-inventor/.claude/skills/aii-web-tools\"; [Learn more.](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/researchers/tools-resources/sharing)\n\nCopy URL\n\nShare a link\n\nShare on\n\n  *  __Email\n  *  __Facebook\n  *  __Twitter\n  *  __LinkedIn\n  *  __Reddit\n  *  __Wechat\n\n\n\n### Abstract\n\nOpen-source software (OSS) is a type of computer software wherein the source code is distributed under a special type of licence in which the copyright holder grants users the rights to inspect, alter, and redistribute the software. OSS projects are collaborative endeavours which have multiple contributors who are constantly joining, leaving, or changing their role in the project. This ever-changing and ever-transient nature of OSS project contributors contributes to a contributor turnover-induced knowledge loss in OSS projects. In this case, \u201cknowledge loss\u201d refers to the phenomenon of the loss of project-specific knowledge, experience, and expertise in an OSS project, caused by contributors regularly joining and leaving the OSS project. This paper describes the design and development of a robust research methodology and contributes towards the formation of proactive knowledge retention practices in OSS projects to transform contributor's use of knowledge and engagement in knowledge-relevant activities including knowledge sharing and knowledge transfer.\n\n##\n\nREFERENCES __\n\n  * 1Clarke P, O'Connor RV, Leavy B. Both candidate prior-art papers are *not* the hypothesis's specific contribution:
- **Hale (2021 thesis)**: Studies *founder decisions* (specifically *software licensing choices*) and their impact on *development-activity resilience after crisis events* (Debian Security Advisories). It does NOT model the *trajectory shape of founder involvement* — the "founder decisions" here are discrete licensing choices, not gradual fade. So while it touches "founder decisions" + resilience, the mechanism and predictor are totally different.
- **Rashid et al. 2020**: Proactive knowledge retention — a *methodological mechanism design* paper about critical incident/knowledge mapping; not a prediction study.

These are tangential and support the hypothesis's novelty claim — no prior work measures *pre-departure fade-curve shape of a single identified founder's involvement as predictor of post-departure survival*. The hypothesis has a genuine, defensible novelty gap.

Let me do a final check on the methodological soundness — specifically checking whether the proposed methodology has *known failure modes* in MSB (red flags: MSB literatureJose exploration of zero-inflated single-founder projects).

Let me focus on synthesizing my findings and writing the review.

Let me summarize the key findings before drafting:

1. **Novelty holds**: No prior work measures the *pre-departure fade-curve shape of a single identified founder's involvement share* as a survival predictor. Closest precedents (Chen et al. ICSE'26, Shock! WWW'24, Avelino ESEM'19) all use *static-at-the-moment* or *post-departure* features. The cross-domain scaffolding transfer is novel for OSS.

2. **Methodological concerns**:
   - **Founder identification fragility**: Avelino itself notes alias resolution is critical; Bock et al. TOSEM 2023 shows unsupervised core-dev identification depends heavily on data source (commits vs issues); Young et al. MSR 2021 shows commit-attribution biases (bots, co-authorship, gender/geographic biases). The "earliest sustained committer = founder" heuristic is an *assumption*, not validated.
   - **Small-sample prediction instability**: Tens to low hundreds of samples with high-dimensional shape descriptors (slope, convexity, plateau-then-cliff indicator, fade index, abrupt-cliff) → CV instability and overfitting; per-ref literature shows small-n CV gives large variance.
   - **Label leakage / self-fulfilling definition**: Departure defined as 12-month inactivity; survival as post-window activity. If founder "gradually fades," the definition of "departure" lands at the *end* of the fade, making the window before it full of declining activity by definition. The "fade" is partly a function of where you place the 12-month cutoff — circular.
   - **Confounding by project type/maturity**: A "scaffold fade" is only possible for projects that had multiple steady contributors already. Projects with TF=1 who slowly leave vs TF=1 who abruptly leave — the gradual one may survive simply because the project was already more diversified, not because of fade. The founder-specific mechanism (assumption 4) confounds with pre-existing community health.
   - **Causality vs prediction**: The proposal mixes predictive modeling with a *mechanistic* claim ("scaffolding fade = capability transfer"). Predictive AUC ≠ causal evidence. The falsification control (non-founder fade) is good but only one confound; doesn't isolate the mechanism.

3. **Citation hygiene**: Honjo & Kato "2022" appears misdated (real Honjo-Kato working paper is ~2020); minor.

4. **Clarity**: Hypothesis is well-organized and Terms section is strong. The "if and only if" in the main claim is very strong (a biconditional) — almost certainly too strong for observational data; should be "predicts" or "is associated with."

5. **Scope**: The sample-size assumption ("tens to low hundreds") may be optimistic given that the cohort must have (a) a clearly identified founder, (b) an actual departure (12-month inactivity), (c) BOTH survival and collapse outcomes, and (d) enough history for trajectory fitting. Avelino found 315 abandoned / 128 survived = 443 total — the hypothesis's achievable cohort is plausibly in the few-hundreds range but the usable cleanly-labeled subset may be much smaller, worsening the small-sample problem.

  "thought": "Let me organize my review around the highest-impact critiques, ranked by score impact:\n\nCRITIQUE 1 (MAJOR \u2014 methodology, would waste compute): The \"if and only if\" biconditional + \"determined by\" framing in the main hypothesis is far too strong for an observational study. Observational data cannot establish biconditional necessity/sufficiency. This sets a falsification bar the study cannot meet and risks an uninformative negative if one counterexample appears. Should weaken to \"predicts\" / \"is associated with.\"\n\nCRITIQUE 2 (MAJOR \u2014 methodology): Founder identification from \"earliest sustained commit/merge activity\" is not validated and the literature (Bock et al. TOSEM 2023; Young et al. MSR 2021; Avelino's own alias-resolution section) shows commit-attribution is fragile (bots, aliases, co-authorship, gender bias, squashed-rebase history loss, founders who migrated from Hg/SVN). The ENTIRE predictor rests on this. Need an explicit validation step + robustness to alternative founder definitions.\n\nCRITIQUE 3 (MAJOR \u2014 methodology, label leakage / circularity): Operating definition of \"departure\" = 12-month inactivity window AND \"survival\" = post-window activity. The fade is measured *up to* departure, but the *location* of the departure cutoff depends on the trajectory. For a project whose founder gradually fades, the 12-month-inactivity line is drawn at the tail of the fade, so the measured curve is partly an artifact of the definition. Need to separate the trajectory measurement window strictly from the labels.\n\nCRITIQUE 4 (MAJOR \u2014 methodology, confounding): The \"scaffolding fade predicts survival\" claim confounds with pre-existing community health/diversification (TF, # contributors). A smooth fade is only POSSIBLE when there are already capable contributors absorbing the founder's share; projects that abruptly leave may simply have lower TF, so the fade is an *epiphenomenon* of prior community health. The non-founder control tests founder-specificity but NOT isolation from static community structure. Need to control for baseline TF / contributor concentration measured at fade onset.\n\nCRITIQUE 5 (MAJOR \u2014 methodology, statistical power): Training predictive models (logistic + survival) on \"tens to low hundreds\" of samples with multiple shape descriptors will have unstable CV-AUC and overfit. The success criterion (\"significantly outperforms static features in cross-validated AUC\") on small-n is unreliable. Need: bootstrap CIs, permutation testing, pre-registered feature set, and report effect size stability across thresholds (which is mentioned but should be primary, not secondary).\n\nCRITIQUE 6 (MAJOR \u2014 scope/feasibility): Sample-size assumption is optimistic. Need founder + genuine departure + both outcomes + sufficient history. Avelino found 443 combined; after applying founder-identification quality + trajectory-fit requirements, usable N may be very small. This directly compounds the small-n problem. Need a power analysis and a fallback plan if N<100.\n\nMINOR critiques:\n- Honjo & Kato \"2022\" appears misdated (real ~2020 working paper). Verify citation.\n- \"explains little of the variation in survival\" slightly overstates Avelino (which does descriptive, not a prediction model against trajectory features).\n- The cross-domain scaffolding transfer is appealing but underspecified as a mechanism \u2014 Vygotsky/Bruner scaffolding involves *structured, intentional* support withdrawal by a tutor; OSS founder fade is plausibly *unwitting* (founder just leaves). The mechanism may be different (passive drift vs active scaffolding), which weakens interpretation of positive results.\n\nLet me also note strengths: genuinely novel angle, careful related-work positioning, clear terms, a built-in falsification control (non-founder fade), multi-channel trajectory (commits+merges+reviews) which is better than commit-only, and sensitivity-analyses across thresholds and label definitions in the design.\n\nOverall: This is a solid, novel, clearly-written hypothesis with a real gap but several methodology-blocking issues that, if unaddressed, could waste compute (the biconditional framing + founder-identification fragility + label leakage are the biggest risks). I'd put it at Weak Accept / Borderline \u2014 score around 6. Soundness 2-3, Presentation 3, Contribution 3-4. Confidence 4.\n\nLet me write the structured JSON now."

```
The review is complete and the structured JSON output has been written to the required path.

## Summary

I reviewed the hypothesis **"Founder Fade Curve Predicts OSS Survival"** as a critical pre-experiment reviewer, grounding every assessment in actual prior art fetched from the web.

### Evidence gathered
- **Confirmed real and accurately characterized**: Avelino et al. (ESEM'19), Chen et al. (ICSE'26), Kaushik & Chahal (JSS'26, May 2026), Noori et al. (2025), the Vygotsky/Wood-Bruner-Ross scaffolding theory.
- **Verified the genuine novelty gap**: No prior work operationalizes a *single identified founder's pre-departure involvement-share trajectory shape* as a survival predictor. Closest precedents (Avelino, "Shock!" WWW'24, Chen et al. ICSE'26, Yin et al. ESEC/FSE'21, Miller et al., Jamieson ICSE'24) all use static-at-the-moment features, community-level post-departure dynamics, or aggregate activity sequences.
- **Flagged two uncited close-in-spirit works**: Yin et al. (ESEC/FSE'21) and Russo Latona et al. ("Shock!" WWW'24).
- **Flagged one unverifiable citation**: "Honjo & Kato 2022" (closest match is a ~2020 working paper).
- **Documented the founder-identification fragility** in the very literature the hypothesis builds on (Avelino's alias-resolution section; Bock et al. TOSEM'23; Young et al. MSR'21).

### Review verdict (written to the JSON)
- **Score: 6/10 (Weak Accept)** — a genuine, well-positioned, novel contribution that needs methodology fixes before compute runs.
- **Confidence: 4/5**.
- **Soundness 2, Presentation 3, Contribution 3.**
- **6 major critiques** (biconditional over-claim, unvalidated founder identification, label leakage/circularity, community-health confound, small-sample power, optimistic sample-size assumption) and **4 minor critiques** (scaffolding mechanism over-claim, Honjo&Kato citation, missing closest prior works, loose Avelino characterization).
- `relation_type = null`, `relation_rationale = ""` (first iteration).

The biggest score-blocking issues, in priority order, are: **(1) the "if and only if"/"determined by" biconditional framing** (observational data can't bear it), **(2) validated founder identification** (the whole predictor hinges on it and the literature documents this is where it breaks), and **(3) departure/survival label leakage** (the gradient fade can be a tautological artifact of the 12-month departure definition). All three are cheap to fix at the hypothesis stage and would each add +0.5–1.0 to the score if addressed.
```
